Proposed Datasets for Pressure Prediction

Area of interest and selection rationale.
My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water
distribution systems, particularly pressure behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using
field measurements and model-based control methods, as described in a published case study. For this mini-project, I would
like to study whether supervised machine learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to forecast future
pressure at a monitored node. The assignment asks for Kaggle datasets with usability above 9. BATADAL satisfies that
criterion with a Kaggle usability of 10.0. BattLeDIM currently shows 3.53 on Kaggle, so it does not meet that specific
threshold; I retain it as a candidate because its original source has substantial academic support, including a peer-reviewed
ASCE benchmark paper, an official research archive, and EPANET network models.
Candidates at a glance
BattLeDIM: L-Town
BATADAL: C-Town

Candidate 1
BattLeDIM: L-Town
Dataset characteristics and academic provenance. BattLeDIM is a simulated SCADA benchmark for L-Town, a
city-scale drinking-water network inspired by a real system in Cyprus. Its Kaggle copy has a usability rating of 3.53, which
is below the assignment’s requested value of greater than 9. I am including it transparently because the underlying dataset
is not an informal Kaggle-only collection: it was developed for an international leakage-detection benchmark, documented
in a peer-reviewed Journal of Water Resources Planning and Management paper, and released with an official Zenodo
archive and EPANET network models. The benchmark paper describes 782 junctions and 905 pipe segments and divides the
network into three hydraulic areas. The SCADA data contain 105,120 five-minute observations in each of 2018 and 2019, for
210,240 observations total. The main measurement table has 119 variables: 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and
82 automated meter reading (AMR) demand measurements. Leakage-event information is also available from the original
benchmark for later diagnostic analysis.
Proposed predictive-learning task. I would use BattLeDIM for supervised regression: predict the recorded pressure
head at one monitored node 15 minutes into the future. Predictors would be limited to information available at forecast
time, such as current and lagged pressures, flows, tank level, AMR demands, and calendar features. I would select the
target sensor after exploratory analysis of topology and pressure variability rather than labeling a node “critical” in advance.
Because 119 candidate variables may be unnecessary, I would compare an all-network baseline with a topology-informed
subset drawn from the same hydraulic area and its boundary connections. Leakage periods could later be used to evaluate
whether forecast errors change under abnormal hydraulic conditions.
Sources: Kaggle
| Official Zenodo archive
| Vrachimis et al. (2022), ASCE


MILESTONE1 PAGE 2 
 Mini-Project Milestone 1
Proposed Datasets for Pressure Prediction
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
Candidate 2
Candidate 2
BATADAL: C-Town
Dataset characteristics and academic provenance. BATADAL is a simulated SCADA benchmark created for the
Battle of the Attack Detection Algorithms on the C-Town water-distribution system. Its Kaggle copy has a usability rating
of 10.0, satisfying the assignment requirement. Like BattLeDIM, it also has strong support beyond Kaggle: the original
challenge provides official datasets, attack-event information, the C-Town EPANET model and control materials, and the
benchmark was documented in a peer-reviewed ASCE paper. C-Town is a medium-sized network with 388 junctions, 429
pipes, seven tanks, 11 pumps, five valves, nine programmable logic controllers, and a SCADA system. The released data are
sampled hourly and contain 43 process variables: seven tank levels, 12 pressures, 12 flows (11 pumps plus one actuated valve),
and 12 binary actuator states. The three principal files contain 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 mixed development
records, and 2,089 test records, for 15,027 observations total.
Proposed predictive-learning task. I would use BATADAL for supervised regression: predict the recorded pressure
head at a selected monitored location one hour ahead. Candidate predictors would include current and lagged tank levels,
flows, actuator states, pressures, and calendar/time features, but not attack labels or any future actuator information. The
hourly sampling interval makes this a different forecasting problem from BattLeDIM and provides a useful comparison of
how temporal resolution affects prediction. I would select the target node after reviewing network topology and pressure
behavior and compare an all-network predictor set with a smaller hydraulically related subset, such as variables from the
same district or from pumps, valves, tanks, and boundary flows that influence the target area. A later error analysis could
separate normal and attack periods; because some BATADAL attacks deliberately falsify SCADA readings, that analysis
would concern the predictability of the recorded signals rather than automatically treating them as true physical pressure.
Sources: Kaggle
| Official BATADAL data
| Taormina et al. (2018), ASCE
Planned evaluation in later stages. For either dataset, I would preserve chronological order when creating training,
validation, and test periods, evaluate forecasts using regression metrics such as MAE and RMSE, and compare learned models
against a simple persistence baseline (future pressure equals current pressure). This provides a clear supervised-learning
framework while avoiding leakage of future information into model training.
Feature scope and dimensionality. The full measurement sets are useful starting points, but I would not assume that
every network variable belongs in the final model. After choosing the target sensor, the EPANET topology can define a
hydraulically related subset—for example nearby pressures, inlet or boundary flows, connected tank levels, relevant pump or
valve states, and local demand information where available. Comparing this physically motivated subset with an all-network
baseline makes feature reduction a testable modeling decision rather than an arbitrary preprocessing step.
Why I am considering these two datasets
Both benchmarks are directly related to pressure behavior in water-distribution systems, contain substantially more observations
than candidate predictors, and include engineering context that can help interpret model inputs and errors. BATADAL fully meets
the stated Kaggle-usability requirement, while BattLeDIM does not; BattLeDIM remains scientifically attractive because of the
quality and reproducibility of its original academic benchmark. Together they frame a clear predictive question for the later stages:
how accurately can future pressure be forecast from information that would actually be available at prediction time?

FEEDBACK:
MILESTONE 2 ASSESSMENT

Description of Dataset and Learning Task: Very Good

Your description of the BattLeDIM dataset is thorough and technically informative. The supplied Zenodo link is valid, and the reported data volume, measurement types, and available variables are consistent with the dataset documentation.

Your proposed task is clearly defined as supervised regression: predicting pressure at n1 fifteen minutes ahead. The selected predictors and proposed construction of time-lagged features are appropriate. The dataset is sufficiently large for the proposed learning methods.

However, Section 1.3 explicitly presents substantial exploratory data analysis (EDA) as your own work, including numerical calculations, correlations, and figures. You also claim to have performed independent checks of the data tables and hydraulic network. No code or other reproducible record is provided to substantiate these claims.

Although performing EDA independently at this stage is permissible, it is not required for Milestone 2. If you choose to present it as original work, its provenance must be clear and verifiable.

Kaggle Contributions: Very Good

You provide a detailed and useful discussion of six relevant studies, including their learning tasks, data preparation, models, code references, evaluation measures, and reported results.

I appreciated your careful distinction between future-pressure forecasting, current-pressure reconstruction, and leak detection. These distinctions are important for determining which previous results are directly comparable with your proposed task.

Your claimed screening of 35 groups of studies is not accompanied by a complete inventory or a reproducible account of the screening process. Since Figure 4 is explicitly presented as your own analysis, you should provide supporting evidence or clarify the source of the information.

Your literature review should also consider Ahmed et al. (2025), "Windowed Multivariate LSTM Autoencoder for Leak Detection in Water Distribution Networks." This study uses the same five principal Area C measurements that you selected, although for a different learning task. Your proposed sixth signal and forecasting objective may still constitute meaningful extensions, but their relationship to existing work should be explained.

Your Anticipated Contributions: Problematic

The proposed supervised learning experiment is clearly specified, and the planned comparison of Gradient Boosting, Ridge, Random Forest, and persistence is technically feasible.

However, there is a serious unresolved issue concerning the originality of the analysis upon which your proposal is based. You repeatedly describe extensive computational results as your own, despite providing no code, notebooks, or other evidence showing how they were obtained.

This makes it difficult to determine which analyses you performed independently, which may have been reproduced or adapted from existing work, and which remain merely proposed. Consequently, the originality of the groundwork supporting your proposed contributions cannot presently be established.

This rating reflects the unresolved questions about attribution and provenance. It is not a determination that an Academic Integrity violation has occurred.

Your proposed contribution also needs a more convincing justification beyond applying established regression models to a narrowly specified forecasting task. Explain precisely what new understanding the experiments are intended to provide, and how the value of your proposed additional predictors will be demonstrated.

Presentation: Problematic

Your report is exceptionally well organized and visually polished. The explanations, tables, figures, and references are generally clear and concise.

However, professional technical reporting requires clear attribution and reproducibility, not merely polished presentation. Labeling figures and numerical results as "Own EDA" without identifying how they were produced is a significant reporting weakness.

The distinction between completed analysis, reproduced analysis, observations drawn from the literature, and proposed future work must be made explicit throughout the report.

Provide appropriate citations for externally obtained results and links to the code supporting any computational work that you claim to have performed.

ADVICE FOR THE NEXT STEPS IN YOUR PROJECT

First, please clarify the provenance of the EDA and other analyses presented as your own. If you performed these analyses independently, provide the corresponding code or notebooks, including the procedures used to generate Figures 2-4, calculate the reported correlations and persistence errors, examine the SCADA tables, and determine the hydraulic path distances.

If any of these results or figures were obtained from existing publications, repositories, tutorials, or other sources, identify those sources explicitly and revise the descriptions so that the authorship of each contribution is accurately represented.

Since Milestone 2 did not require implementation, you will not be penalized for work that has not yet been performed. It is important, however, not to represent anticipated work or externally obtained results as independently completed analysis.

For your final report, retain the useful discussion of previous models, but strengthen the explanation of how your contribution differs from existing studies. In particular, distinguish the novelty of the forecasting task, feature selection, experimental comparisons, and evaluation methodology.

Your reported persistence baseline already achieves a very small error. This suggests that improving on persistence may be difficult. Determine whether Gradient Boosting provides a meaningful improvement, and whether the additional signals improve prediction beyond what can be achieved using n1 history alone.

Ensure that all model comparisons use identical forecast targets, evaluation periods, and performance measures. Preserve chronological ordering, prevent future information from entering the predictors, and avoid selecting model settings using the final test period.

The proposed experiments are manageable for a four-week group project, provided that the group concentrates on reproducible data preparation, a small number of well-justified models, and carefully controlled comparisons.

Please proceed to Milestone 3 by advertising your proposal and forming a group. Before developing this proposal into the introductory material for your final report, however, the questions concerning the provenance and originality of the claimed analyses must be resolved.

- Michael Houle

```text
"""Checks, past/future alignment, descriptive EDA and nominal pipe-graph distances."""
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path
import heapq
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import yaml

SIGNALS = ['n1', 'n4', 'n31', 'T1', 'PUMP_1', 'AMR_n1']
TARGET = 'n1_plus15'
PERIOD_START = '2018-01-01'
PERIOD_END = '2018-09-30'


def read_and_check_tables(raw):
    """2019 is read for structural checks only; no 2019 prediction score is computed."""
    raw = Path(raw)
    structural = []
    training = {}
    for year in [2018, 2019]:
        previous_index = None
        for kind, columns in [('Pressures', 33), ('Flows', 3), ('Levels', 1), ('Demands', 82)]:
            name = f'{year}_SCADA_{kind}.csv'
            frame = pd.read_csv(raw / name, sep=';', decimal=',', index_col=0, parse_dates=True)
            if frame.shape != (105120, columns):
                raise ValueError(f'Unexpected shape: {name}: {frame.shape}')
            assert frame.index.is_unique and frame.index.is_monotonic_increasing
            assert frame.index.to_series().diff().dropna().eq(pd.Timedelta(minutes=5)).all()
            assert not frame.isna().any().any()
            assert frame.index[0] == pd.Timestamp(f'{year}-01-01 00:00')
            assert frame.index[-1] == pd.Timestamp(f'{year}-12-31 23:55')
            if previous_index is not None:
                assert frame.index.equals(previous_index), f'Timestamp mismatch: {name}'
            previous_index = frame.index
            structural.append({
                'filename': name, 'year': year, 'records': len(frame), 'features': columns,
                'duplicate_timestamps': 0, 'missing_values': 0, 'spacing_minutes': 5,
                'timestamps_match_other_tables_in_year': True,
                'use': 'structure_and_Jan_Sep_EDA' if year == 2018 else 'structure_only',
            })
            if year == 2018:
                training[kind] = frame.loc[PERIOD_START:PERIOD_END].copy()
            print(f'Checked {name}: {len(frame):,} rows, {columns} channels', flush=True)
    pressures = training['Pressures']
    x = pressures[['n1', 'n4', 'n31']].join(training['Levels'][['T1']])
    x = x.join(training['Flows'][['PUMP_1']])
    x = x.join(training['Demands'][['n1']].rename(columns={'n1': 'AMR_n1'}))
    assert list(x.columns) == SIGNALS and x.shape == (78624, 6)
    return x, structural


def make_forecast_pairs(x, steps=3):
    """Crop the period first, then shift: the final 3 labels cannot cross September."""
    if steps < 1:
        raise ValueError('The forecast lead must be positive.')
    assert x.index.is_unique and x.index.is_monotonic_increasing
    pairs = x.copy()
    pairs[TARGET] = x['n1'].shift(-steps)
    pairs = pairs.dropna()
    return pairs


def describe(x):
    pairs = make_forecast_pairs(x)
    error = pairs[TARGET] - pairs['n1']
    corr = pairs.corr(method='pearson')
    statistics = {
        'period_start': PERIOD_START, 'period_end': PERIOD_END, 'days': 273,
        'rows': len(x), 'forecast_pairs': len(pairs), 'lead_minutes': 15,
        'n1_range_m': [float(x.n1.min()), float(x.n1.max())],
        'persistence_MAE_m': float(error.abs().mean()),
        'persistence_RMSE_m': float(np.sqrt((error ** 2).mean())),
        'correlation_with_future_n1': {name: float(corr.loc[name, TARGET]) for name in SIGNALS},
        'signals': SIGNALS, 'target': TARGET,
        'interpretation': 'Descriptive Jan-Sep 2018 results; not a final 2019 test score.',
        'learned_models_fitted': False,
    }
    assert len(pairs) == 78621
    return statistics, corr, pairs


def parse_inp(path):
    sections = {}
    section = None
    for line in Path(path).read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig').splitlines():
        text = line.split(';', 1)[0].strip()
        if text.startswith('['):
            section = text.upper()
            sections[section] = []
        elif text and section:
            sections[section].append(text.split())
    return sections


def nominal_topology(raw):
    """Undirected, initially open pipe graph; excludes pumps and PRVs as boundaries."""
    raw = Path(raw)
    sections = parse_inp(raw / 'L-TOWN.inp')
    config = yaml.safe_load((raw / 'dataset_configuration.yaml').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    closed = []
    for row in sections['[PIPES]']:
        if len(row) > 7 and row[7].upper() == 'CLOSED':
            closed.append(row[0])
            continue
        left, right, length = row[1], row[2], float(row[3])
        graph[left].append((right, length, row[0]))
        graph[right].append((left, length, row[0]))

    def component(root):
        seen = set()
        stack = [root]
        while stack:
            node = stack.pop()
            if node in seen:
                continue
            seen.add(node)
            stack.extend(peer for peer, _, _ in graph[node] if peer not in seen)
        return seen

    distances = {}
    paths = {}
    queue = [(0.0, 'T1', ())]
    while queue:
        distance, node, pipes = heapq.heappop(queue)
        if node in distances:
            continue
        distances[node] = distance
        paths[node] = list(pipes)
        for peer, length, pipe in graph[node]:
            if peer not in distances:
                heapq.heappush(queue, (distance + length, peer, pipes + (pipe,)))

    junctions = {row[0] for row in sections['[JUNCTIONS]']}
    sectors = {}
    components = {}
    for area, root in [('A', 'n300'), ('B', 'n226'), ('C', 'T1')]:
        nodes = component(root)
        components[area] = nodes
        sensors = [node for node in config['pressure_sensors'] if node in nodes]
        sectors[area] = {
            'junctions': len(nodes & junctions), 'pressure_sensors': sensors,
            'pressure_sensor_count': len(sensors), 'AMR_count': len(set(config['amrs']) & nodes),
        }
    assert not any(components[a] & components[b] for a, b in [('A', 'B'), ('A', 'C'), ('B', 'C')])
    upstream = sorted(junctions - set.union(*components.values()))
    assert sum(s['junctions'] for s in sectors.values()) + len(upstream) == 782
    measured_distances = {node: {'distance_m': distances[node], 'pipe_path': paths[node]} for node in ['n1', 'n4', 'n31']}
    return {
        'model': 'L-TOWN.inp (nominal)', 'algorithm': 'Dijkstra shortest total pipe length from T1',
        'scope': 'Initially open undirected pipes; pumps and PRVs excluded from sector connections.',
        'network_counts': {name: len(sections[key]) for name, key in [
            ('junctions', '[JUNCTIONS]'), ('reservoirs', '[RESERVOIRS]'), ('tanks', '[TANKS]'),
            ('pipes', '[PIPES]'), ('pumps', '[PUMPS]'), ('valves', '[VALVES]')]},
        'sectors': sectors, 'upstream_boundary_junctions': upstream, 'closed_pipes_excluded': closed,
        'T1_to_measured_pressure_nodes': measured_distances,
        'PRVs': [{'id': r[0], 'from': r[1], 'to': r[2], 'type': r[4], 'nominal_setting_m': float(r[5])} for r in sections['[VALVES]']],
        'nominal_controls': [' '.join(row) for row in sections['[CONTROLS]']],
        'feature_filter_counts': {'all_channels': 119, 'Area_C_and_pump_candidates': 87, 'selected_signals': 6},
        'limitations': [
            'Pipe-path length is not hydraulic sensitivity, pressure propagation delay or travel time.',
            'Nominal setpoints do not establish observed time-varying valve states.',
            'A tank supply boundary does not prove statistical independence from upstream conditions.',
            'PUMP_1 is tank inflow; AMR_n1 is nodal consumption, not sector inflow.',
        ],
    }

"""Acquire the fixed Zenodo release and validate its published checksums."""
from pathlib import Path
import hashlib
import json
import shutil
import time
import urllib.request

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]


def digest(path, algorithm='sha256'):
    h = hashlib.new(algorithm)
    with Path(path).open('rb') as handle:
        for block in iter(lambda: handle.read(1024 * 1024), b''):
            h.update(block)
    return h.hexdigest()


def source_manifest():
    return json.loads((ROOT / 'data/source_manifest.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))


def ensure_data(folder, download=False, import_folder=None):
    """Do not silently substitute a different mirror or dataset release."""
    folder = Path(folder)
    folder.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    manifest = source_manifest()
    records = []
    for source in manifest['files']:
        path = folder / source['filename']
        method = 'existing_local_file_checked_against_official_md5'
        if not path.exists() and import_folder is not None:
            candidate = Path(import_folder) / source['filename']
            if candidate.exists():
                shutil.copyfile(candidate, path)
                method = 'imported_local_cache_checked_against_official_md5'
        if not path.exists():
            if not download:
                raise FileNotFoundError(
                    f'{source["filename"]} is missing. Run python reproduce.py --download '
                    'or specify --import-data /path/to/flat/cache.'
                )
            print(f'Downloading {source["filename"]} from Zenodo ...', flush=True)
            temporary = path.with_name(path.name + '.part')
            for attempt in range(3):
                try:
                    request = urllib.request.Request(
                        source['download_url'], headers={'User-Agent': 'DS675-reproducibility/1.0'}
                    )
                    with urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=45) as response, temporary.open('wb') as output:
                        shutil.copyfileobj(response, output)
                    if digest(temporary, 'md5') != source['md5']:
                        raise ValueError(f'Official checksum mismatch: {source["filename"]}')
                    temporary.replace(path)
                    break
                except Exception:
                    if temporary.exists():
                        temporary.unlink()
                    if attempt == 2:
                        raise
                    time.sleep(1)
            method = 'downloaded_from_official_zenodo_url'
        actual = digest(path, 'md5')
        if actual != source['md5']:
            raise ValueError(f'Checksum mismatch for {source["filename"]}: {actual}')
        records.append({
            'filename': path.name, 'acquisition': method, 'bytes': path.stat().st_size,
            'md5': actual, 'official_md5_matches': True, 'sha256': digest(path),
            'source_url': source['download_url'], 'license': 'CC-BY-4.0',
        })
        print(f'Verified {path.name}', flush=True)
    return records

```

```text
"""Regenerate the report's descriptive figures using portable Matplotlib fonts."""
import numpy as np
import matplotlib
matplotlib.use('Agg')
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from matplotlib import colors
from matplotlib.patches import Patch

NAVY = '#12324A'
TEAL = '#16697A'
GOLD = '#E9A23B'


def style():
    plt.rcParams.update({
        'font.family': 'DejaVu Serif', 'font.size': 9, 'text.color': NAVY,
        'axes.labelcolor': NAVY, 'xtick.color': NAVY, 'ytick.color': NAVY,
        'axes.spines.top': False, 'axes.spines.right': False,
    })


def save(fig, folder, name):
    for extension in ['png', 'svg']:
        fig.savefig(folder / f'{name}.{extension}', dpi=240, facecolor='white')
    plt.close(fig)


def daily_profiles(x, folder):
    style()
    fig = plt.figure(figsize=(10.4, 3.68))
    bottom, top, gap = .13, .87, .085
    span = top - bottom
    left_height = (span - gap) / 2
    right_height = (span - 2 * gap) / 3
    axes = {
        'T1': fig.add_axes([.075, top-left_height, .365, left_height]),
        'PUMP_1': fig.add_axes([.075, bottom, .365, left_height]),
        'n1': fig.add_axes([.60, top-right_height, .365, right_height]),
        'n4': fig.add_axes([.60, top-2*right_height-gap, .365, right_height]),
        'n31': fig.add_axes([.60, bottom, .365, right_height]),
    }
    palette = [TEAL, '#2A9D8F', GOLD, NAVY]
    for name, ax in axes.items():
        daily = x[[name]].assign(day=x.index.date, slot=x.index.hour*12+x.index.minute//5)
        matrix = daily.pivot(index='day', columns='slot', values=name)
        assert matrix.shape == (273, 288) and not matrix.isna().any().any()
        hours = np.arange(288) / 12
        for number, row in enumerate(matrix.to_numpy()):
            ax.plot(hours, row, color=palette[number % 4], alpha=.14, lw=.35)
        ax.plot(hours, matrix.mean(axis=0), color='#A81738', lw=1.7)
        ax.set_xlim(0, 24)
        ax.set_xticks([0, 6, 12, 18, 24], ['00:00', '06:00', '12:00', '18:00', '24:00'])
        ax.tick_params(labelsize=8, pad=2, length=2.3)
        ax.grid(alpha=.16)
        ax.margins(y=.08)
        label = 'T1 level (m)' if name == 'T1' else 'PUMP_1 (m$^3$/h)' if name == 'PUMP_1' else name+' pressure (m)'
        ax.set_ylabel(label, fontsize=9, labelpad=4)
        if name == 'PUMP_1':
            ax.set_ylim(bottom=0)
        if name in ['PUMP_1', 'n31']:
            ax.set_xlabel('Time of day (hh:mm)', fontsize=9, labelpad=3)
        else:
            ax.tick_params(axis='x', labelbottom=False)
    fig.legend([plt.Line2D([0], [0], color='#A81738', lw=1.7)],
               ['Clock-time mean; thin lines = 273 observed days'], loc='upper center',
               bbox_to_anchor=(.53, 1.005), frameon=False, fontsize=10)
    save(fig, folder, 'figure2_daily_profiles')


def correlations(corr, folder):
    style()
    fig = plt.figure(figsize=(8.9, 3.12))
    ax = fig.add_axes([.29, .19, .47, .77])
    cmap = colors.LinearSegmentedColormap.from_list('correlations', [NAVY, '#EDF5F7', '#2A9D8F'])
    im = ax.imshow(corr, vmin=-1, vmax=1, cmap=cmap, aspect='equal')
    labels = ['n1', 'n4', 'n31', 'T1', 'PUMP_1', 'AMR_n1', 'n1 +15']
    ax.set_xticks(range(7), labels, fontsize=10, rotation=32, ha='right')
    ax.set_yticks(range(7), labels, fontsize=10)
    ax.tick_params(length=0, pad=4)
    for i in range(7):
        for j in range(7):
            value = corr.iloc[i, j]
            ax.text(j, i, f'{value:.2f}', ha='center', va='center', fontsize=8.5,
                    color='white' if abs(value) > .68 else NAVY)
    ax.axhline(5.5, color=GOLD, lw=1.5)
    ax.axvline(5.5, color=GOLD, lw=1.5)
    cax = fig.add_axes([.705, .26, .014, .59])
    fig.colorbar(im, cax=cax, ticks=[-1, -.5, 0, .5, 1], label='Pearson r')
    cax.tick_params(labelsize=9)
    save(fig, folder, 'figure3_correlations')


def screening(summary, folder):
    style()
    rows = summary['categories']
    fig = plt.figure(figsize=(7.18, 1.40), facecolor='white')
    ax = fig.add_axes([.065, .29, .92, .40])
    positions = list(range(len(rows)))
    ax.set_axisbelow(True)
    ax.grid(axis='y', color='#E4EBEE', linewidth=.5)
    ax.bar(positions, [row['groups'] for row in rows], width=.62, color=TEAL)
    ax.bar(positions, [row['selected'] for row in rows], width=.24, color=GOLD)
    labels = ['Leaks /\nlocalization', 'Current-state\nestimation', 'Pressure/head\nforecasting',
              'Anomalies /\nsensor faults', 'Sensor\nplacement']
    ax.set_xticks(positions, [label+'\n'+str(row['selected'])+' reviewed' for label, row in zip(labels, rows)])
    ax.tick_params(axis='x', length=0, pad=2, labelsize=7.7)
    ax.set_yticks([0, 15, 30])
    ax.tick_params(axis='y', length=0, pad=3, labelsize=7.4)
    ax.set_ylabel('Groups', fontsize=7.7, labelpad=3)
    ax.set_ylim(0, max(row['groups'] for row in rows)+8)
    ax.set_xlim(-.65, len(rows)-.35)
    for spine in ax.spines.values():
        spine.set_visible(False)
    for position, row in zip(positions, rows):
        ax.text(position, row['groups']+1, f"{row['groups']} ({row['percent']:.1f}%)",
                ha='center', va='bottom', fontsize=8, color=NAVY)
    total = summary['groups']
    fig.text(.005, .99, f'{total} groups of related works on L-Town', va='top',
             fontsize=9, fontweight='bold', color=NAVY)
    fig.legend(handles=[Patch(color=TEAL, label=f'Eligible groups (N={total})'),
                        Patch(color=GOLD, label=f"Selected for comparison (n={summary['selected_groups']})")],
               loc='upper left', bbox_to_anchor=(.06, .865), ncol=2, frameon=False,
               fontsize=8, handlelength=1.4, columnspacing=2, borderaxespad=0)
    save(fig, folder, 'figure4_historical_screening')

"""Recount the published historical inventory; do not claim to repeat a web search."""
import csv
from .data import ROOT

CATEGORIES = [('leak', 'Leak detection / localization'), ('state', 'Current-state estimation'),
              ('forecast', 'Pressure/head forecasting'), ('anomaly', 'Anomalies / sensor faults'),
              ('sensors', 'Sensor placement')]


def recount():
    with (ROOT / 'literature/inventory_sources.csv').open(encoding='utf-8-sig', newline='') as handle:
        sources = list(csv.DictReader(handle))
    with (ROOT / 'literature/group_assignments.csv').open(encoding='utf-8-sig', newline='') as handle:
        groups = list(csv.DictReader(handle))
    mapping = {group['group']: group for group in groups}
    assert len(mapping) == len(groups), 'Duplicate group identifier'
    assert {row['group'] for row in sources} == set(mapping), 'Inventory/group mismatch'
    for row in sources:
        assert row['category'] == mapping[row['group']]['category']
        assert row['selected'].lower() == mapping[row['group']]['selected'].lower()
    categories = []
    for key, label in CATEGORIES:
        selected = [g for g in groups if g['category'] == key]
        categories.append({'category': key, 'label': label, 'groups': len(selected),
                           'percent': 100*len(selected)/len(groups),
                           'selected': sum(g['selected'].lower() == 'true' for g in selected),
                           'group_ids': [g['group'] for g in selected]})
    assert sum(row['groups'] for row in categories) == len(groups)
    return {
        'source_records': len(sources), 'groups': len(groups),
        'selected_groups': sum(g['selected'].lower() == 'true' for g in groups),
        'categories': categories,
        'status': 'Reproduction of the submitted-report inventory, not an exhaustive systematic review.',
        'attribution': 'Literature scope, selection and interpretation by Andres Arango Martinez; AI support for retrieval, organization and counting code under his direction.',
        'Ahmed_2025_in_historical_inventory': False,
        'limitation': 'Code verifies grouping/count consistency, not the completeness of search or correctness of every scholarly classification.',
    }

"""Single reproducible entry point for the feedback's computational evidence requests."""
import argparse
from datetime import datetime, timezone
import importlib.metadata
import json
from pathlib import Path
import platform
import sys
import numpy as np
from . import analysis, figures, screening
from .data import ROOT, digest, ensure_data


def dump(path, value):
    path.write_text(json.dumps(value, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)+'\n', encoding='utf-8')


def run(data_dir, output_dir, download=False, import_data=None):
    output = Path(output_dir)
    output.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    figure_dir = output / 'figures'
    figure_dir.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    inputs = ensure_data(data_dir, download=download, import_folder=import_data)
    x, structure = analysis.read_and_check_tables(data_dir)
    stats, corr, pairs = analysis.describe(x)
    topology = analysis.nominal_topology(data_dir)
    counts = screening.recount()
    dump(output / 'table_checks.json', structure)
    dump(output / 'eda_statistics.json', stats)
    dump(output / 'topology_audit.json', topology)
    dump(output / 'screening_summary.json', counts)
    corr.to_csv(output / 'correlations.csv', index_label='variable')
    x.head(20).to_csv(output / 'selected_inputs_sample.csv', index_label='forecast_origin')
    pairs.head(20).to_csv(output / 'forecast_pairs_sample.csv', index_label='forecast_origin')
    x.to_csv(output / 'selected_inputs_full.csv.gz', index_label='forecast_origin', compression='gzip')
    pairs.to_csv(output / 'forecast_pairs_full.csv.gz', index_label='forecast_origin', compression='gzip')
    figures.daily_profiles(x, figure_dir)
    figures.correlations(corr, figure_dir)
    figures.screening(counts, figure_dir)

    # Expected values are fixed historical report claims, independent of this refactor.
    expected = json.loads((ROOT / 'provenance/expected_report_values.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
    matches = []
    for key in ['rows', 'forecast_pairs', 'days']:
        assert stats[key] == expected[key], (key, stats[key], expected[key])
        matches.append(key)
    for key in ['persistence_MAE_m', 'persistence_RMSE_m']:
        assert abs(stats[key]-expected[key]) < 1e-12, (key, stats[key], expected[key])
        matches.append(key)
    assert np.allclose(stats['n1_range_m'], expected['n1_range_m'], atol=1e-12)
    for name, value in expected['correlation_with_future_n1'].items():
        assert abs(stats['correlation_with_future_n1'][name]-value) < 1e-10
    for name, distance in expected['T1_pipe_distances_m'].items():
        actual = topology['T1_to_measured_pressure_nodes'][name]['distance_m']
        assert round(actual) == distance, (name, actual, distance)
        assert abs(actual-expected['T1_pipe_distances_unrounded_m'][name]) < 1e-8
    assert counts['source_records'] == 47 and counts['groups'] == 35
    assert [r['groups'] for r in counts['categories']] == [27, 3, 2, 2, 1]
    assert topology['network_counts'] == expected['network_counts']
    assert topology['sectors']['C']['junctions'] == 92
    assert topology['sectors']['C']['pressure_sensors'] == ['n1', 'n4', 'n31']
    assert topology['sectors']['C']['AMR_count'] == 82
    checks = {'expected_numeric_claims_match': True, 'table_checks_pass': True,
              'reported_2018_correlations_match': True, 'reported_pipe_distances_match': True,
              'historical_inventory_counts_match': True, 'models_trained': False,
              '2019_use': 'Structural checks only; no predictive result computed.',
              'figure_reproduction': 'Same source values and plotting procedure; portable font, not a claim of pixel identity.',
              'research_attribution': 'Research design, analyses and decisions by Andres Arango Martinez; assisted code implements his instructions.'}
    dump(output / 'verification.json', checks)
    output_hashes = {str(path.relative_to(output)).replace('\\', '/'): digest(path)
                     for path in sorted(output.rglob('*')) if path.is_file() and path.name != 'run_manifest.json'}
    code_hashes = {str(path.relative_to(ROOT)).replace('\\', '/'): digest(path)
                   for path in sorted((ROOT / 'repro').glob('*.py'))}
    code_hashes['reproduce.py'] = digest(ROOT / 'reproduce.py')
    classification_inputs = {name: digest(ROOT / name) for name in [
        'literature/inventory_sources.csv', 'literature/group_assignments.csv',
        'data/source_manifest.json', 'provenance/expected_report_values.json']}
    manifest = {
        'run_utc': datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(),
        'python': sys.version, 'platform': platform.platform(),
        'packages': {n: importlib.metadata.version(n) for n in ['numpy', 'pandas', 'matplotlib', 'PyYAML']},
        'inputs': inputs, 'code_sha256': code_hashes, 'classification_and_protocol_sha256': classification_inputs,
        'output_sha256': output_hashes,
        'results_status': 'Executed descriptive reproduction; no learned models trained.',
        'authorship': 'Research objective, signals, comparisons, analyses and interpretation by Andres Arango Martinez. AI-supported code generation and execution under his instructions; external data/methods credited separately.',
    }
    dump(output / 'run_manifest.json', manifest)
    print(json.dumps({'status': 'PASS', 'rows': stats['rows'], 'pairs': stats['forecast_pairs'],
                      'MAE_m': stats['persistence_MAE_m'], 'RMSE_m': stats['persistence_RMSE_m'],
                      'groups': counts['groups'], 'models_trained': False}, indent=2), flush=True)
    return stats, topology, counts


def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)
    parser.add_argument('--data-dir', type=Path, default=ROOT / 'data/raw')
    parser.add_argument('--output-dir', type=Path, default=ROOT / 'results/reproduced')
    parser.add_argument('--download', action='store_true', help='Download missing official files and verify their published MD5.')
    parser.add_argument('--import-data', type=Path, help='Optional flat cache of the exact official files; every checksum is checked.')
    args = parser.parse_args()
    run(args.data_dir, args.output_dir, args.download, args.import_data)

Crawled: last month; Volume 27, September 2025, 106911 ... This study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective.\n\n## Results in Engineering\n\nVolume 27, September 2025, 106911\n\n# \n\nResearch paper\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nAttention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\n\n  * •\n\nModel is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\n\n  * •\n\nModel exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\n\n  * •\n\nModel explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\n\n## Abstract\n\nEfficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\nDerived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. The following sections detail the challenges addressed and present the explainable AGN framework.\n\n### 2.1. Problem formulation\n\nBefore discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph 𝒢=(𝒱,ℰ,𝒲). Here, 𝒱 is the set of 𝐼+𝐼* vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. ℰ is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. \nGiven the nodal pressures 𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽=⎡⎢ ⎢⎣𝑝𝑣1,𝑡1⋯𝑝𝑣1,𝑡𝐽⋮⋱⋮𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡1⋯𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽⎤⎥ ⎥⎦∈ℝ𝐼×𝐽 at 𝐼 vertices of historical 𝐽 time steps, nodal heights 𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼∈ℝ𝐼 and nodal types 𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼∈ℝ𝐼 at 𝐼 vertices, the training task is to forecast next 𝐽* time steps demand, denoted as ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽*=⎡⎢ ⎢⎣ℎ𝑣1,𝑡1⋯ℎ𝑣1,𝑡𝐽*⋮⋱⋮ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡1⋯ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽*⎤⎥ ⎥⎦∈ℝ𝐼×𝐽*, that achieves trustworthy approximations of the ground truths ̂ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽*, which is given by:(3)𝑇⁢𝑟⁢𝑎⁢𝑖⁢𝑛⁢𝑖⁢𝑛⁢𝑔⁡(𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽+𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼)=𝑎⁢𝑟⁢𝑔⁡𝑚⁢𝑖⁢𝑛Θ⁢1𝐽*⁢𝐽+𝐽*∑𝐽∣ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽*−̂ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽*∣where Θ denotes all learnable parameters in the AGN model. It is crucial to emphasize that assuming inputs from pressure sensors and calculating losses based on all nodes is impractical in real situations. That is, nodes to be generalized (i.e. non-sensor nodes) cannot be considered known during training to prevent information leakage [19,33]. Thus, despite the aim of this study is to achieve prediction of all nodes, the input dimension of ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽 in Eq. (3) is 𝐼×𝐽, instead of (𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽.\n\nThe training challenge lies in learning the transferable correlation between sensor measurements and nodal features. After acquiring the trained model of the 𝐼 sensor nodes, forecasting all 𝐼+𝐼* nodes are accomplished by additionally inputting the 𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼* and 𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼* of the remaining 𝐼* non-sensor nodes:(4)𝐺⁡𝑒⁢𝑛⁢𝑒⁢𝑟⁢𝑎⁢𝑙⁢𝑖⁢𝑧⁢𝑖⁢𝑛⁢𝑔⁡(𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽+𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐼*+𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼+𝐼*)=𝑓Θ⁡(𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽+𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐼*+𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼+𝐼*)=ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐼*,𝑡𝐽*\n\n\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\n\nImage: Fig 1\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (318KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.\n\n(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) 𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 with the weighted adjacency matrix 𝒲𝑣𝑖, the past CHPS of sensor node 𝑣𝑖 is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:((5))̅𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝒲𝑣𝑖·𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 where 𝑙 is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS 𝑐(0)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 is an initialized learnable parameter.\n\n(2) Long-short-term memory augmented by self-attention mechanism: By calculating the matrix product of the query vector 𝑄·⁡(·) and the key vector 𝐾·⁡(·) of the node sequence (Eqs. (6)-a and 7-a), the attention between node sequences can be reflected. The attent…795 tokens truncated… primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. \n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1009KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 7. Nodal features analysis: (a) Mask nodal type and nodal elevation features; (b) Mask nodal type features; (c) Mask nodal elevation features.\n\nAdding any individual nodal feature to the “AGN(-type)(-height)” scenario can significantly enhance the model’s overall prediction accuracy. Specifically, adding only the nodal height feature (Fig. 7(b)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<1.0 m from 0 % to 95.6 %. This indicates that height, a key indicator of spatial location information, provides extensive and fundamental predictive support for the model. Adding nodal type features (Fig. 7(c)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<0.5 m from 0 % to 23.2 %. This significantly enhances the model’s ability to capture specific nodal characteristics and improves prediction accuracy, demonstrating the unique value of type features in refining predictions. Both nodal elevation and type features offer distinct and complementary contributions to model performance. The results highlight the critical role of prior network information in improving model performance.\n\nHowever, in water networks lacking detailed nodal information, obtaining accurate nodal height and type data can be challenging. Therefore, promoting and deepening the AGN model in practical applications will require diversified model assistance and data substitution strategies. For instance, nodal height can be approximated using surface elevation, and nodal type can be inferred by integrating urban AOI data with network topology. With the inclusion and integration of more network prior information, the AGN model can better align with water network characteristics, becoming powerful tools for the safety management and distribution optimization of WDSs.\n\n\nExplainable AI and Machine Learning for Water Resources Engineering: Enhancing Decision-Making and Sustainable Development through Interpretable Models in Civil Engineering and Spatial Sciences\n\nEdited by Dr. Komali Kantamaneni (University of Central Lancashire, Preston, , United Kingdom), Professor Upaka Rathnayake (Atlantic Technological University - Sligo, Sligo, , Ireland), Assist. In this work, we combine physics-based modeling and Graph Neural Networks (GNN), a data-driven approach, to address the pressure estimation problem.\nTitle: Graph Neural Networks for Pressure Estimation in Water Distribution Systems\nAuthors: Huy Truong, Andrés Tello, Alexander Lazovik, Victoria Degeler\nDate: Fri Nov 17 15:30:12 2023\n\nPressure and flow estimation in Water Distribution Networks (WDN) allows water management companies to optimize their control operations. For many years, mathematical simulation tools have been the most common approach to reconstructing an estimate of the WDN hydraulics. However, pure physics-based simulations involve several challenges, e.g. partially observable data, high uncertainty, and extensive manual configuration. Thus, data-driven approaches have gained traction to overcome such limitations. In this work, we combine physics-based modeling and Graph Neural Networks (GNN), a data-driven approach, to address the pressure estimation problem. First, we propose a new data generation method using a mathematical simulation but not considering temporal patterns and including some control parameters that remain untouched in previous works; this contributes to a more diverse training data. Second, our training strategy relies on random sensor placement making our GNN-based estimation model robust to unexpected sensor location changes. Third, a realistic evaluation protocol considers real temporal patterns and additionally injects the uncertainties intrinsic to real-world scenarios. Finally, a multi-graph pre-training strategy allows the model to be reused for pressure estimation in unseen target WDNs. Our GNN-based model estimates the pressure of a large-scale WDN in The Netherlands with a MAE of 1.94mH$_2$O and a MAPE of 7%, surpassing the performance of previous studies. Likewise, it outperformed previous approaches on other WDN benchmarks, showing a reduction of absolute error up to approximately 52% in the best cases.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nUniversity of Groningen (https://pure.rug.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/1397555398/A83_1_.pdf)\nciteturn130search13 [wordlim: 200] Published: 4 months ago; Windowed Multivariate LSTM Autoencoder for Leak Detection in Water Distribution Networks\nAhmed, Samer; Dustegor, Dilek; Lazovik, Alexander; Wörtche, Heinrich J. ...  Wang_2022_ASCE.pdf pages 12 Multiple Leakage Detection and Isolation in District
Metering Areas Using a Multistage Approach
Xiaoting Wang1; Junyu Li2; Shuming Liu3; Xipeng Yu4; and Ziqing Ma5
Abstract: Hydraulic accidents or abnormal situations, also known as leakages, cause not only water losses but also service interruptions and
other negative effects. To facilitate the rapid response of water utilities and reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely,
efficient, and accurate detection method is required. This paper describes a novel framework for multiple overlapping leak detection and
isolation in the water distribution system. The proposed method has three stages: an estimation stage to extract leak-induced data from the
huge monitoring data, an identification stage to detect the presence of overlapping leaks from multiscale difference series, and a localization
stage to pinpoint the leak hotspots. The proposed method detects seven leakages in real-life networks and localizes 16 overlapping leakages
with an economic score of €167,981 in L-town 2019. Comparisons show that the framework can accurately detect it in a fast response time
and effectively localize the leaking pipeline with good applicability. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001558. © 2022 American
Society of Civil Engineers.
Author keywords: Data-driven approach; Hydraulic model; Empirical mode decomposition; Time series decomposition; Multiscale
difference operation.
Introduction
Leak detection in water distribution systems (WDSs) is a great
challenge for worldwide water utilities. Serious leakage accidents
cause not only water losses but also significantly negative effects,
such as the abrupt release of pressure and the intrusion of contam-
inants through broken pipes (Wu et al. 2018; Xu et al. 2018). The
key to burst detection is to reduce the awareness time, i.e., the
period between the burst occurrence and the awareness of water
utilities. In short, timely leak detection is of vital importance for
water utilities.
To detect leakages in WDSs in time, quantities of equipment
have been applied for leak detection in the water industry, which
mainly includes manual listening stick, standard correlator, and
correlating noise loggers (Mutikanga et al. 2013; Shimanskiy et al.
2003). Although the mentioned equipment can localize leakage
precisely, it remains a great challenge when facing the cost of time,
money, and human power, and consequently resulting in a heavy
burden on water utilities (Li et al. 2015).
Compared with the presented methods relying on hardware
equipment, detection based on the monitoring data in district me-
tering areas (DMAs) costs less and arouses wide concern in the
water industry for its convenience. The monitoring data reflect the
operating states of the network. When leaks happen, the operating
patterns of monitoring data will be different from the ones under
normal conditions. By mining the DMA monitoring data, leakage
can be identified in real-time (Romano et al. 2014). The data-driven
method can be mainly divided into three types, including statistical
process control (SPC), classification, and prediction-classification
two-stage method (Wu and Liu 2017b). Jung et al. (2015) propose
and demonstrate the application of several SPC methods on de-
tecting leaks. Pressure and flow data are analyzed through standard
deviation, cumulative sum (CUSUM), and threshold settings to
detect the abnormal statistical status caused by leakages. These
SPC-based methods have been considered as simple, concise, and
direct techniques for leak detection (Ahn and Jung 2019). Some
researchers treat leakage detection as a binary classification prob-
lem. By training a classification model based on machine learning,
the monitoring data are divided into normal and leak (Machell et al.
2006). Furthermore, since the raising of the artificial neural net-
work (ANN) prediction model for networks (Mounce et al. 2002),
the two-stage method based on the ANNs has been a research focus
in the water supply industry. This method employs historical data
such as pressure and flow to train the prediction model and then
classifies according to the deviation between predicted and mon
```

# Historical search, screening and grouping

## What is reproducible

The committed CSVs reproduce **Figure 4 of the submitted report**: 47 source records assigned to
35 related-work groups, with categories 27 leak/localization, 3 current-state estimation,
2 forecasting, 2 anomaly/fault detection and 1 sensor placement. Six groups were selected
for detailed discussion. Papers, versions and author repositories describing one method
are grouped rather than counted independently.

`inventory_sources.csv` retains every source ID, original URL, evidence locator and saved
checksum/commit from the historical inventory. `group_assignments.csv` makes all group
IDs, categories and selected/not-selected decisions visible. `repro/screening.py` joins the
files and recomputes counts. The code does not contain the category totals as its counting rule.

## Historical collection strategy

Andres Arango Martinez directed source discovery, relevance screening and focused reading,
with AI retrieval, organization and coding support. The [review method](REVIEW_METHOD_AND_STUDENT_ROLE.md)
and [six-study matrix](SIX_STUDY_CRITICAL_MATRIX.md) distinguish the author's scholarly
judgments from automated counting and from external authors' model results.

The earlier local search record was an exploratory, AI-assisted literature collection,
not a systematic review or a Scopus/Web of Science bibliometric study. It started from
the BattLeDIM committee, official dataset, benchmark paper and competition reports;
followed citations and author code; then searched for pressure prediction/reconstruction,
graphs, autoencoders, dictionaries, ensembles and related evaluations.

Representative queries retained from that earlier search record include:

- `BattLeDIM L-Town leak detection localization github benchmark`
- `L-Town pressure prediction forecasting github BattLeDIM`
- `"BattLeDIM" "pressure forecasting"`
- `"L-Town" "pressure" "forecasting" learning`
- `"BattLeDIM" "15-minute" forecasting`
- `"BattLeDIM" "github" "autoencoder"`
- `"SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning" pdf`

The counted set retains original or explicitly modified L-Town studies. Other networks,
general reviews and tools are not in the historical denominator. A method's primary goal
determines its category even if pressure forecasting is an intermediate diagnostic step.
This is a scholarly classification, not a result established by counting code.

## Limits and post-feedback addition

Search engines and publisher availability change; a complete historical result log was
not retained. The CSV joins verify counting and grouping consistency; the author's classification and interpretation are scholarly judgments, not outputs validated by those joins alone. The inventory does not establish worldwide priority.

**Ahmed et al. (2025), DOI 10.15131/shef.data.29921204.v1, was missing from the historical
inventory.** Its PDF/link is listed as a post-feedback addition in the literature index; it
is not silently inserted into the 35-group historical count. A future literature revision
must reassess grouping and selection in light of that paper.
